I mean, I don't have an issue paying attention to things, and I can follow instructions just fine. Like, if someone tells me how to do a specific scan at work or gives me an assignment, I get it done without a problem. It's really just the physical part... like, having to stay in one spot. My focus itself is actually okay, I just can't sit still while I'm doing it.\n\nDoctor: When did you first start feeling this need to move around and inability to sit still?\n\nPatient: Honestly, it's been going on for a really long time. Um, my parents actually still talk about my elementary school conferences. They said teachers were always bringing up how I was constantly out of my seat, calling out, or just... rushing ahead before they even finished giving directions. So, yeah, this was happening way before middle school. It's definitely not something that just started with my radiology training.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do that a lot. Um, like at work, when a supervisor is explaining a positioning step or asking me a question about a scan, I'll just jump in and blurt out the answer before they even finish talking. And then, you know, a second later I realize I actually missed the part of the sentence that mattered. So, yeah, that definitely happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you often do things impulsively, like interrupting tasks or activities to do something else?\n\nPatient: Um, no, I wouldn't say I do that. Once I start a task, I usually just get it done. Like, if I'm setting up the room for an X-ray or something, I don't just drop it in the middle to go do something completely different. So, no, I don't really have a problem with abandoning my activities like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk excessively, to the point where others have trouble getting a word in?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I know I just said I interrupt people sometimes, but I don't think I talk excessively. I'm not really the type to just go on and on to the point where other people can't speak. So, no, I haven't noticed that being an issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty organizing tasks and activities?\n\nPatient: Um, no, I wouldn't say I have trouble organizing things. My job is pretty structured, you know? I get a patient, I check the orders, I set up the equipment, and I do the scan. It's a clear step-by-step process, so I don't really struggle with figuring out what to do or getting it all organized. I just follow the routine.\n\nDoctor: Did you exhibit excessive running, climbing, or physical activity as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I did. My parents actually still talk about my elementary school conferences. The teachers always said I was constantly out of my seat, calling out, or just... rushing ahead to do things before they even finished giving the directions. So, yeah, that was happening way before middle school. It's definitely not just something that started recently with my radiology training.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of restlessness and inability to sit still caused you any problems in your relationships or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely has. Um, mainly with my training. My clinical evaluations have really taken a hit lately. I was actually just put on a formal remediation plan. The supervisors told me that my behavior... well, they said it disrupts the patient flow and makes teamwork a lot harder for everyone else. I'm honestly really worried that it could cost me my place in the program if I don't figure out how to get it under control.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant distress because of your symptoms, beyond the issues you've mentioned with your radiology training?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Honestly, the main issue is just the radiology program. Outside of that, I don't feel like I'm in distress or anything. I mean, my friends are used to me being energetic, and it doesn't really bother me personally. It's just... the hospital setting requires a certain vibe, and that's where I'm getting into trouble. But outside of work? No, I wouldn't say I'm distressed.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant problems with sustaining attention on tasks or play activities in childhood or currently?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really had an issue with that. I mean, my problem has always been more about not being able to sit still or keep quiet. When it comes to actually paying attention to a task, I do fine. I don't zone out or lose focus like that, neither when I was a kid nor now. It's just the physical restlessness that gets me in trouble.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel impatient or have difficulty waiting for others?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Waiting is... really hard for me. Like, during lab practice or when I'm waiting for a machine to open up, I get so edgy. I just can't stand there. I keep stepping forward, checking the time, and asking if I can go next. It's actually gotten me called out more than once by the instructors because I just can't seem to wait my turn.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. So, yeah, that definitely happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you often do things impulsively, like interrupting tasks or activities to do something else?\n\nPatient: Um, no, I wouldn't say I do that. Once I start a task, I usually just get it done. Like, if I'm setting up the room for an X-ray or something, I don't just drop it in the middle to go do something completely different. So, no, I don't really have a problem with abandoning my activities like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk excessively, to the point where others have trouble getting a word in?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I know I just said I interrupt people sometimes, but I don't think I talk excessively. I'm not really the type to just go on and on to the point where other people can't speak. So, no, I haven't noticed that being an issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty organizing tasks and activities?\n\nPatient: Um, no, I wouldn't say I have trouble organizing things. My job is pretty structured, you know? I get a patient, I check the orders, I set up the equipment, and I do the scan. It's a clear step-by-step process, so I don't really struggle with figuring out what to do or getting it all organized. I just follow the routine.\n\nDoctor: Did you exhibit excessive running, climbing, or physical activity as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I did. My parents actually still talk about my elementary school conferences. The teachers always said I was constantly out of my seat, calling out, or just... rushing ahead to do things before they even finished giving the directions. So, yeah, that was happening way before middle school. It's definitely not just something that started recently with my radiology training.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of restlessness and inability to sit still caused you any problems in your relationships or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely has. Um, mainly with my training. My clinical evaluations have really taken a hit lately. I was actually just put on a formal remediation plan. The supervisors told me that my behavior... well, they said it disrupts the patient flow and makes teamwork a lot harder for everyone else. I'm honestly really worried that it could cost me my place in the program if I don't figure out how to get it under control.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant distress because of your symptoms, beyond the issues you've mentioned with your radiology training?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Honestly, the main issue is just the radiology program. Outside of that, I don't feel like I'm in distress or anything. I mean, my friends are used to me being energetic, and it doesn't really bother me personally. It's just... the hospital setting requires a certain vibe, and that's where I'm getting into trouble. But outside of work? No, I wouldn't say I'm distressed.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant problems with sustaining attention on tasks or play activities in childhood or currently?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really had an issue with that. I mean, my problem has always been more about not being able to sit still or keep quiet. When it comes to actually paying attention to a task, I do fine. I don't zone out or lose focus like that, neither when I was a kid nor now. It's just the physical restlessness that gets me in trouble.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel impatient or have difficulty waiting for others?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Waiting is... really hard for me. Like, during lab practice or when I'm waiting for a machine to open up, I get so edgy. I just can't stand there. I keep stepping forward, checking the time, and asking if I can go next. It's actually gotten me called out more than once by the instructors because I just can't seem to wait my turn.\n\nDoctor: Did you have more energy than your peers as a child, always being on the go?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I did. My parents actually still bring up my elementary school parent-teacher conferences. The teachers were always telling them that I was constantly out of my seat, calling out answers, or just rushing ahead to do things before they even finished giving the directions. So, yeah, that kind of energy and always being on the go was happening way before middle school, not just since I started my radiology training.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms affected your personal relationships or friendships outside of work?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I guess my friends know I'm always moving around or whatever, but it hasn't caused any real problems with them. We just kind of hang out and do our thing. I haven't really noticed it messing up my personal life or my friendships or anything like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It happens a lot at work in the radiology suite, or when I'm in a classroom. I’ve been trying to just tough it out for months now, but I still end up pacing, shifting around in my seat, or just finding some random excuse to get up and move. It's just getting really hard to deal with.\n\nDoctor: Does this restlessness happen only at work or in classrooms, or do you experience it in other situations as well?\n\nPatient: Um, I don't think it's just there. It honestly feels like it happens pretty much everywhere. I haven't really kept track of every single place or anything, but... yeah, basically anytime I'm expected to just sit down and not move, I get that same feeling. So it's definitely not just at work or in class.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have trouble paying attention or following through on instructions when you're in these situations where you feel the need to move?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I mean, I don't have an issue paying attention to things, and I can follow instructions just fine. Like, if someone tells me how to do a specific scan at work or gives me an assignment, I get it done without a problem. It's really just the physical part... like, having to stay in one spot. My focus itself is actually okay, I just can't sit still while I'm doing it.\n\nDoctor: When did you first start feeling this need to move around and inability to sit still?\n\nPatient: Honestly, it's been going on for a really long time. Um, my parents actually still talk about my elementary school conferences. They said teachers were always bringing up how I was constantly out of my seat, calling out, or just... rushing ahead before they even finished giving directions. So, yeah, this was happening way before middle school. It's definitely not something that just started with my radiology training.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do that a lot. Um, like at work, when a supervisor is explaining a positioning step or asking me a question about a scan, I'll just jump in and blurt out the answer before they even finish talking. And then, you know, a second later I realize I actually missed the part of the sentence that mattered. So, yeah, that definitely happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you often do things impulsively, like interrupting tasks or activities to do something else?\n\nPatient: Um, no, I wouldn't say I do that. Once I start a task, I usually just get it done. Like, if I'm setting up the room for an X-ray or something, I don't just drop it in the middle to go do something completely different. So, no, I don't really have a problem with abandoning my activities like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk excessively, to the point where others have trouble getting a word in?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I know I just said I interrupt people sometimes, but I don't think I talk excessively. I'm not really the type to just go on and on to the point where other people can't speak. So, no, I haven't noticed that being an issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty organizing tasks and activities?\n\nPatient: Um, no, I wouldn't say I have trouble organizing things. My job is pretty structured, you know? I get a patient, I check the orders, I set up the equipment, and I do the scan. It's a clear step-by-step process, so I don't really struggle with figuring out what to do or getting it all organized. I just follow the routine.\n\nDoctor: Did you exhibit excessive running, climbing, or physical activity as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I did. My parents actually still talk about my elementary school conferences. The teachers always said I was constantly out of my seat, calling out, or just... rushing ahead to do things before they even finished giving the directions. So, yeah, that was happening way before middle school. It's definitely not just something that started recently with my radiology training.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of restlessness and inability to sit still caused you any problems in your relationships or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely has. Honestly, the main issue is just the radiology program. Outside of that, I don't feel like I'm in distress or anything. I mean, my friends are used to me being energetic, and it doesn't really bother me personally. It's just... the hospital setting requires a certain vibe, and that's where I'm getting into trouble. But outside of work? No, I wouldn't say I'm distressed.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant problems with sustaining attention on tasks or play activities in childhood or currently?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really had an issue with that. I mean, my problem has always been more about not being able to sit still or keep quiet. When it comes to actually paying attention to a task, I do fine. I don't zone out or lose focus like that, neither when I was a kid nor now. It's just the physical restlessness that gets me in trouble.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel impatient or have difficulty waiting for others?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Waiting is... really hard for me. Like, during lab practice or when I'm waiting for a machine to open up, I get so edgy. I just can't stand there. I keep stepping forward, checking the time, and asking if I can go next. It's actually gotten me called out more than once by the instructors because I just can't seem to wait my turn.\n\nDoctor: Did you have more energy than your peers as a child, always being on the go?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I did. My parents actually still bring up my elementary school parent-teacher conferences.           "difficulty waiting for others"
        ],
        "count": 2
      }
    ],
    "duration_verified": "symptoms present since childhood, with a history of similar behavior in elementary school",
    "functional_impairment": true,
    "traumatic_stressor": null,
    "psychosocial_stressor": null,
    "additional_requirements": [
      "onset prior to age 12 confirmed",
      "symptoms present in two or more settings (e.g., work, classroom)"
    ]
  }
}
